(c) Explain the phenomenon of quantum tunnelling. State and explain the   (4)
mathematical expression of tunnelling probability of a particle in a potential
barrier. Also, identify the factors that increase the tunnelling probability.
Solution:
When both the width L and the height U0 of a potential barrier are finite, a part of
the quantum wave packet incident on one side of the barrier can penetrate the
barrier boundary and continue its motion inside the barrier, where it is gradually
attenuated on its way to the other side. A part of the incident quantum wave packet
eventually emerges on the other side of the barrier in the form of the transmitted
wave packet that tunneled through the barrier. This is termed as quantum tunneling.
---------------- (1 mark)
The tunneling probability T(L,E) is obtained as
where
Where E is the energy of the particle and U0 is the height of the potential barrier.
-------------------- (2 marks)
Tunneling probability depends on the barrier width and its height, and on the
energy of the quantum particle incident on the barrier.
---------------- (1 mark)
(d) Explain the concept of (a) macrostate, (b) microstate, (c) phase space and (d)   (4)
statistical ensemble.
Solution:
The macroscopic state or the macrostate of a system can be described by the
macroscopic parameters and the energy of the system such as temperature,
pressure, volume, density, etc.
---------------- (1 mark)
********** 2 **********
--- page 3 ---
The microscopic state or the microstate (also known as quantum state) of a system
can be specified by quoting the microscopic states such as position, momentum,
orientation, and configuration of the individual particles.
---------------- (1 mark)
This 6 numerical quantities required to specify the state of a particle in space at a
particular instant of time is defined as phase space.
---------------- (1 mark)
If we observe a system for a considerable period of time, recording its state at a
number of equal intervals of time (the number tending to infinity), we shall have a
collection of phase points. These phase points are supposed to represent an
identical system with the same macroscopic properties, but different microscopic
states. Thus we have a (infinitely) large number of systems similar in structure but
suitably randomized in microstates. This collection of imagined systems, which are
similar (i.e. macroscopically identical) but essentially independent and non-
interacting, is called ensemble.
---------------- (1 mark)
(e)   Explain (a) degrees of freedom with proper example (b) law of equipartition of   (4)
energy.
Solution:
The total number of independent quantities required to specify the configuration
and position of a system is called the number of degrees of freedom of the system.
The number is determined by the possibilities of motion of the parts of the system.
In the case of a gas, if we regard it as a point, its position is specified by the
coordinates x, y and z. thus each gas particle will have three degrees of freedom.
---------------- (2 marks)
The law of equipartition of energy states that the thermal equilibrium energy of the
gas particles is uniformly distributed among the various degrees of freedom and
for each of them it is (½)kT.
---------------- (2 marks)
Q.2.1   (20)
(i).(a) Define photoelectric effect and state all its five experimental results.   (10)
The work function for cadmium is 4.08 eV. What must be the wavelength of
radiation incident on cadmium so that the maximum velocity of photoelectrons
will be 7.2 x 105 m/s.
Solution:
A series of experiments performed in the late nineteenth century revealed that
electrons are emitted from a metal surface when light of sufficiently large
frequency (excepting alkali metals all other metals require ultraviolet rays) falls
upon it. This phenomenon is known as the photoelectric effect.
---------------- (1 mark)
The experimental results can be summarized as
1. It is an instantaneous process. The time lag between the incidence of light and
emission of electrons is ~ 10-9 s.
2. There is a threshold frequency for the emission of photoelectrons. If the light
beam contains no photons having frequencies higher than this threshold, no
********** 3 **********
--- page 4 ---
photoelectron will be emitted (in other words, there will be no photo current)
regardless of the intensity of the light source.
3. The threshold frequency varies with different metals.
4. The magnitude of the photocurrent is independent of the frequency of the light
but is directly proportional to the intensity of the light. However, the average
electron energy remains same.
5. For a given material, the higher the frequency of the light, the more energy the
photoelectrons have and the value of stopping potential also increases.
---------------- (5 marks)

(iii).(b) Explain “the half-life of polonium that emits 8-MeV α-particles is only hundreds
of milliseconds and the half-life of uranium that emits 4-MeV α-particles is   (6)
billions of years” by applying quantum mechanical tunnelling.
Solution:
Gamow explained the variation of half-lives by considering a ‘spherical-box’
model of the nucleus, where α-particles can bounce back and forth between the
walls as free particles. The confinement is provided by a strong nuclear potential
at a spherical wall of the box. The thickness of this wall, however, is not infinite
but finite, so in principle, a nuclear particle has a chance to escape this nuclear
confinement. On the inside wall of the confining barrier is a high nuclear potential
that keeps the α-particle in a small confinement. But when an α-particle gets out to
the other side of this wall, it is subject to electrostatic Coulomb repulsion and
moves away from the nucleus.
-------------------- (1 mark)
----------------- (1 mark for diagram)
The width L of the potential barrier that separates an α-
particle from the outside world depends on the particle’s
kinetic energy E. This width is the distance between the
point marked by the nuclear radius R, and the point, R0,
where an α-particle emerges on the other side of the barrier
-------------------- (1 mark)
At the distance the kinetic energy of the α-particle must
be equal to electrostatic Coulomb repulsion energy.
2𝑍𝑒2
𝐸=
4𝜋𝜀0𝑅0
********** 8 **********
--- page 9 ---
-------------------- (1 mark)
Substituting the value of R0
2𝑍𝑒2
𝐿= 4𝜋𝜀0𝐸−𝑅
------------------------ (1 mark)
Hence, higher the energy of α-particle, the narrower the width of the barrier that
it is to tunnel through. Also, the width of the potential barrier is the most
important parameter in tunneling probability. Thus, highly energetic α-particles
have a good chance to escape the nucleus, and, for such nuclei, the nuclear
disintegration half-life is short. However, this process is highly nonlinear,
meaning a small increase in the α-particle energy can exponentially increase the
tunneling probability. This explains why the half-life of polonium that emits 8-
MeV α-particles is only hundreds of milliseconds and the half-life of uranium
that emits 4-MeV α-particles is billions of years.
------------------------ (1 mark)

(iv).(b)   Describe microcanonical and grand canonical ensemble with the help of suitable   (6)
diagrams.
Solution:
Microcanonical ensemble: It is the collection of a large number of systems having
the same energy E, volume V and total number of particles N. This ensemble is
also often called the NVE ensemble, as each of these three quantities is a constant
of the ensemble. The individual systems of microcanonical ensemble must remain
totally isolated (i.e. they are separated by rigid, impermeable and well insulated
walls, unable to exchange energy or particles with its environment) in order to stay
in statistical equilibrium.
------------------------ (3 marks= 2marks for explanation + 1 mark for diagram)
Grand Canonical ensemble: It is an ensemble of a large number of essentially
independent systems having the same temperature T, volume V and chemical
potential 𝜇. In the grand canonical ensemble, both energy E and number of particle
N are variable. The individual systems of a grand canonical ensemble are separated
by rigid, permeable but conducting walls as shown in Fig. 4. As the separating
walls are conducting and permeable, the exchange of heat energy as well as the
particles between the systems takes place, bringing all the systems arrive at
common temperature T and chemical potential 𝜇. The chemical potential is the
energy required to add a particle to the system.
------------------------ (3 marks= 2marks for explanation + 1 mark for diagram)

Let us divide a large box into k cells having areas aj, a2, a3, ..., ax (Fig. 5). Then we throw n

balls. This is a distribution. If this experiment is repeated many times, it is found that certain
distribution occurs more frequently than others. This particular outcome is the most probable
distribution.

The probability W of a particular distribution depends on two factors:

i. The a priori probability (G) of the distribution which depends on the property of each
cell.
ii. The thermodynamic probability (Q) of the distribution which is the number of

different accessible sequences in which the balls may be distributed among the cells
without changing the number of balls in each cell. Thus we assume that the balls are
identical but distinguishable.

Q.2.6   (20)
(vi).(a) State the four basic postulates of Maxwell-Boltzmann statistics. Write the   (10)
expression for Maxwell-Boltzmann distribution function. Give any two
applications and any two limitations of Maxwell-Boltzmann statistics.
Solution:
The basic postulates of MB statistics are
i. The associated particles are identical but distinguishable.
ii. Each energy state can contain any number of particles.
iii. Total number of particles in the entire system is constant.
iv. Total energy of all the particles in the entire system is constant.
v. Examples: gas molecules at high temperature and low pressure.

Above Eqn. is the Planck’s radiation formula in terms of frequency v, and has been successful
in explaining the black body radiation.
--- page 15 ---
------------------------ (2 marks)
Any two applications of MB statistics   ------------------------- (2 marks)
The MB statistics is applicable to the ensembles of particles forming a dilute gas
(where the average separation between the molecules is large, individual molecules
could be distinguished) and also to free electrons in the conduction band of a
semiconductor at ordinary temperatures, provided the doping of the semiconductor
is not too high.
Any two limitations of MB statistics   ------------------------- (2 marks)
1. This statistics is applicable only to isolated gas molecular system in equilibrium,
where the potential energy due to the mutual interaction between the molecules is
negligible compared to their mean kinetic energy and the gas is dilute.
2. The expression for MB-count does not lead to the correct expression for entropy of an
ideal gas. It leads to what is known as Gibbs paradox, which can be resolved by dividing
the expression by N!.

(c) Explain the phenomenon of quantum tunnelling. State and explain the   (4)
mathematical expression of tunnelling probability of a particle in a potential
barrier. Also, identify the factors that increase the tunnelling probability.
Solution:
When both the width L and the height U0 of a potential barrier are finite, a part of
the quantum wave packet incident on one side of the barrier can penetrate the
barrier boundary and continue its motion inside the barrier, where it is gradually
attenuated on its way to the other side. A part of the incident quantum wave packet
eventually emerges on the other side of the barrier in the form of the transmitted
wave packet that tunneled through the barrier. This is termed as quantum tunneling.
---------------- (1 mark)
The tunneling probability T(L,E) is obtained as
where
Where E is the energy of the particle and U0 is the height of the potential barrier.
-------------------- (2 marks)
Tunneling probability depends on the barrier width and its height, and on the
energy of the quantum particle incident on the barrier.
---------------- (1 mark)
(d) Explain the concept of (a) macrostate, (b) microstate, (c) phase space and (d)   (4)
statistical ensemble.
Solution:
The macroscopic state or the macrostate of a system can be described by the
macroscopic parameters and the energy of the system such as temperature,
pressure, volume, density, etc.
---------------- (1 mark)
********** 2 **********
--- page 3 ---
The microscopic state or the microstate (also known as quantum state) of a system
can be specified by quoting the microscopic states such as position, momentum,
orientation, and configuration of the individual particles.
---------------- (1 mark)
This 6 numerical quantities required to specify the state of a particle in space at a
particular instant of time is defined as phase space.
---------------- (1 mark)
If we observe a system for a considerable period of time, recording its state at a
number of equal intervals of time (the number tending to infinity), we shall have a
collection of phase points. These phase points are supposed to represent an
identical system with the same macroscopic properties, but different microscopic
states. Thus we have a (infinitely) large number of systems similar in structure but
suitably randomized in microstates. This collection of imagined systems, which are
similar (i.e. macroscopically identical) but essentially independent and non-
interacting, is called ensemble.
---------------- (1 mark)
(e)   Explain (a) degrees of freedom with proper example (b) law of equipartition of   (4)
energy.
Solution:
The total number of independent quantities required to specify the configuration
and position of a system is called the number of degrees of freedom of the system.
The number is determined by the possibilities of motion of the parts of the system.
In the case of a gas, if we regard it as a point, its position is specified by the
coordinates x, y and z. thus each gas particle will have three degrees of freedom.
---------------- (2 marks)
The law of equipartition of energy states that the thermal equilibrium energy of the
gas particles is uniformly distributed among the various degrees of freedom and
for each of them it is (½)kT.
---------------- (2 marks)
Q.2.1   (20)
(i).(a) Define photoelectric effect and state all its five experimental results.   (10)
The work function for cadmium is 4.08 eV. What must be the wavelength of
radiation incident on cadmium so that the maximum velocity of photoelectrons
will be 7.2 x 105 m/s.
Solution:
A series of experiments performed in the late nineteenth century revealed that
electrons are emitted from a metal surface when light of sufficiently large
frequency (excepting alkali metals all other metals require ultraviolet rays) falls
upon it. This phenomenon is known as the photoelectric effect.
---------------- (1 mark)
The experimental results can be summarized as
1. It is an instantaneous process. The time lag between the incidence of light and
emission of electrons is ~ 10-9 s.
2. There is a threshold frequency for the emission of photoelectrons. If the light
beam contains no photons having frequencies higher than this threshold, no
********** 3 **********
--- page 4 ---
photoelectron will be emitted (in other words, there will be no photo current)
regardless of the intensity of the light source.
3. The threshold frequency varies with different metals.
4. The magnitude of the photocurrent is independent of the frequency of the light
but is directly proportional to the intensity of the light. However, the average
electron energy remains same.
5. For a given material, the higher the frequency of the light, the more energy the
photoelectrons have and the value of stopping potential also increases.
---------------- (5 marks)
Problem:
Given: W = 4.08 eV = 4.08 x 1.6 x 10-19 = 6.528 x 10-19 J
v = 7.2 x 105 m/s
m = 9.11 × 10−31 kg

(iii).(b) Explain “the half-life of polonium that emits 8-MeV α-particles is only hundreds
of milliseconds and the half-life of uranium that emits 4-MeV α-particles is   (6)
billions of years” by applying quantum mechanical tunnelling.
Solution:
Gamow explained the variation of half-lives by considering a ‘spherical-box’
model of the nucleus, where α-particles can bounce back and forth between the
walls as free particles. The confinement is provided by a strong nuclear potential
at a spherical wall of the box. The thickness of this wall, however, is not infinite
but finite, so in principle, a nuclear particle has a chance to escape this nuclear
confinement. On the inside wall of the confining barrier is a high nuclear potential
that keeps the α-particle in a small confinement. But when an α-particle gets out to
the other side of this wall, it is subject to electrostatic Coulomb repulsion and
moves away from the nucleus.
-------------------- (1 mark)
----------------- (1 mark for diagram)
The width L of the potential barrier that separates an α-
particle from the outside world depends on the particle’s
kinetic energy E. This width is the distance between the
point marked by the nuclear radius R, and the point, R0,
where an α-particle emerges on the other side of the barrier
-------------------- (1 mark)
At the distance the kinetic energy of the α-particle must
be equal to electrostatic Coulomb repulsion energy.
2𝑍𝑒2
𝐸=
4𝜋𝜀0𝑅0
********** 8 **********
--- page 9 ---
-------------------- (1 mark)
Substituting the value of R0
2𝑍𝑒2
𝐿= 4𝜋𝜀0𝐸−𝑅
------------------------ (1 mark)
Hence, higher the energy of α-particle, the narrower the width of the barrier that
it is to tunnel through. Also, the width of the potential barrier is the most
important parameter in tunneling probability. Thus, highly energetic α-particles
have a good chance to escape the nucleus, and, for such nuclei, the nuclear
disintegration half-life is short. However, this process is highly nonlinear,
meaning a small increase in the α-particle energy can exponentially increase the
tunneling probability. This explains why the half-life of polonium that emits 8-
MeV α-particles is only hundreds of milliseconds and the half-life of uranium
that emits 4-MeV α-particles is billions of years.

(iv).(b)   Describe microcanonical and grand canonical ensemble with the help of suitable   (6)
diagrams.
Solution:
Microcanonical ensemble: It is the collection of a large number of systems having
the same energy E, volume V and total number of particles N. This ensemble is
also often called the NVE ensemble, as each of these three quantities is a constant
of the ensemble. The individual systems of microcanonical ensemble must remain
totally isolated (i.e. they are separated by rigid, impermeable and well insulated
walls, unable to exchange energy or particles with its environment) in order to stay
in statistical equilibrium.
------------------------ (3 marks= 2marks for explanation + 1 mark for diagram)
Grand Canonical ensemble: It is an ensemble of a large number of essentially
independent systems having the same temperature T, volume V and chemical
potential 𝜇. In the grand canonical ensemble, both energy E and number of particle
N are variable. The individual systems of a grand canonical ensemble are separated
by rigid, permeable but conducting walls as shown in Fig. 4. As the separating
walls are conducting and permeable, the exchange of heat energy as well as the
particles between the systems takes place, bringing all the systems arrive at
common temperature T and chemical potential 𝜇. The chemical potential is the
energy required to add a particle to the system.
------------------------ (3 marks= 2marks for explanation + 1 mark for diagram)

(v).(a)   Find the mathematical formulation for the probability W of distribution for a   (10)

Let us divide a large box into k cells having areas aj, a2, a3, ..., ax (Fig. 5). Then we throw n
identical balls such that no part of the box is favored. Each cell will contain different number of
balls. This is a distribution. If this experiment is repeated many times, it is found that certain
distribution occurs more frequently than others. This particular outcome is the most probable
distribution.

The probability W of a particular distribution depends on two factors:

i. The a priori probability (G) of the distribution which depends on the property of each
cell.
ii. The thermodynamic probability (Q) of the distribution which is the number of

different accessible sequences in which the balls may be distributed among the cells
without changing the number of balls in each cell. Thus we assume that the balls are
identical but distinguishable.

indistinguishable and behave as a system of non-interacting particles.

To count the photons, we have to estimate the number of cells in the phase space (constructed
by positions and momentum of all photons). If each cell has the infinitesimal volume h’, then
the number of cells in the phase space where the momentum lies between p and p + dp is

Q.2.6   (20)
(vi).(a) State the four basic postulates of Maxwell-Boltzmann statistics. Write the   (10)
expression for Maxwell-Boltzmann distribution function. Give any two
applications and any two limitations of Maxwell-Boltzmann statistics.
Solution:
The basic postulates of MB statistics are
i. The associated particles are identical but distinguishable.
ii. Each energy state can contain any number of particles.
iii. Total number of particles in the entire system is constant.
iv. Total energy of all the particles in the entire system is constant.
v. Examples: gas molecules at high temperature and low pressure.
------------------------ (Any four postulates=4 marks)

a

Above Eqn. is the Planck’s radiation formula in terms of frequency v, and has been successful
in explaining the black body radiation.
--- page 15 ---
------------------------ (2 marks)
Any two applications of MB statistics   ------------------------- (2 marks)
The MB statistics is applicable to the ensembles of particles forming a dilute gas
(where the average separation between the molecules is large, individual molecules
could be distinguished) and also to free electrons in the conduction band of a
semiconductor at ordinary temperatures, provided the doping of the semiconductor
is not too high.
Any two limitations of MB statistics   ------------------------- (2 marks)
1. This statistics is applicable only to isolated gas molecular system in equilibrium,
where the potential energy due to the mutual interaction between the molecules is
negligible compared to their mean kinetic energy and the gas is dilute.
2. The expression for MB-count does not lead to the correct expression for entropy of an
ideal gas. It leads to what is known as Gibbs paradox, which can be resolved by dividing
the expression by N!.

(c) Explain the phenomenon of quantum tunnelling. State and explain the   (4)
mathematical expression of tunnelling probability of a particle in a potential
barrier. Also, identify the factors that increase the tunnelling probability.
Solution:
When both the width L and the height U0 of a potential barrier are finite, a part of
the quantum wave packet incident on one side of the barrier can penetrate the
barrier boundary and continue its motion inside the barrier, where it is gradually
attenuated on its way to the other side. A part of the incident quantum wave packet
eventually emerges on the other side of the barrier in the form of the transmitted
wave packet that tunneled through the barrier. This is termed as quantum tunneling.
---------------- (1 mark)
The tunneling probability T(L,E) is obtained as
where
Where E is the energy of the particle and U0 is the height of the potential barrier.
-------------------- (2 marks)
Tunneling probability depends on the barrier width and its height, and on the
energy of the quantum particle incident on the barrier.
---------------- (1 mark)
(d) Explain the concept of (a) macrostate, (b) microstate, (c) phase space and (d)   (4)
statistical ensemble.
Solution:
The macroscopic state or the macrostate of a system can be described by the
macroscopic parameters and the energy of the system such as temperature,
pressure, volume, density, etc.
---------------- (1 mark)
********** 2 **********
--- page 3 ---
The microscopic state or the microstate (also known as quantum state) of a system
can be specified by quoting the microscopic states such as position, momentum,
orientation, and configuration of the individual particles.
---------------- (1 mark)
This 6 numerical quantities required to specify the state of a particle in space at a
particular instant of time is defined as phase space.
---------------- (1 mark)
If we observe a system for a considerable period of time, recording its state at a
number of equal intervals of time (the number tending to infinity), we shall have a
collection of phase points. These phase points are supposed to represent an
identical system with the same macroscopic properties, but different microscopic
states. Thus we have a (infinitely) large number of systems similar in structure but
suitably randomized in microstates. This collection of imagined systems, which are
similar (i.e. macroscopically identical) but essentially independent and non-
interacting, is called ensemble.
---------------- (1 mark)
(e)   Explain (a) degrees of freedom with proper example (b) law of equipartition of   (4)
energy.
Solution:
The total number of independent quantities required to specify the configuration
and position of a system is called the number of degrees of freedom of the system.
The number is determined by the possibilities of motion of the parts of the system.
In the case of a gas, if we regard it as a point, its position is specified by the
coordinates x, y and z. thus each gas particle will have three degrees of freedom.
---------------- (2 marks)
The law of equipartition of energy states that the thermal equilibrium energy of the
gas particles is uniformly distributed among the various degrees of freedom and
for each of them it is (½)kT.
---------------- (2 marks)
Q.2.1   (20)
(i).(a) Define photoelectric effect and state all its five experimental results.   (10)
The work function for cadmium is 4.08 eV. What must be the wavelength of
radiation incident on cadmium so that the maximum velocity of photoelectrons
will be 7.2 x 105 m/s.
Solution:
A series of experiments performed in the late nineteenth century revealed that
electrons are emitted from a metal surface when light of sufficiently large
frequency (excepting alkali metals all other metals require ultraviolet rays) falls
upon it. This phenomenon is known as the photoelectric effect.
---------------- (1 mark)
The experimental results can be summarized as
1. It is an instantaneous process. The time lag between the incidence of light and
emission of electrons is ~ 10-9 s.
2. There is a threshold frequency for the emission of photoelectrons. If the light
beam contains no photons having frequencies higher than this threshold, no
********** 3 **********
--- page 4 ---
photoelectron will be emitted (in other words, there will be no photo current)
regardless of the intensity of the light source.
3. The threshold frequency varies with different metals.
4. The magnitude of the photocurrent is independent of the frequency of the light
but is directly proportional to the intensity of the light. However, the average
electron energy remains same.
5. For a given material, the higher the frequency of the light, the more energy the
photoelectrons have and the value of stopping potential also increases.
---------------- (5 marks)

(iii).(b) Explain “the half-life of polonium that emits 8-MeV α-particles is only hundreds
of milliseconds and the half-life of uranium that emits 4-MeV α-particles is   (6)
billions of years” by applying quantum mechanical tunnelling.
Solution:
Gamow explained the variation of half-lives by considering a ‘spherical-box’
model of the nucleus, where α-particles can bounce back and forth between the
walls as free particles. The confinement is provided by a strong nuclear potential
at a spherical wall of the box. The thickness of this wall, however, is not infinite
but finite, so in principle, a nuclear particle has a chance to escape this nuclear
confinement. On the inside wall of the confining barrier is a high nuclear potential
that keeps the α-particle in a small confinement. But when an α-particle gets out to
the other side of this wall, it is subject to electrostatic Coulomb repulsion and
moves away from the nucleus.
-------------------- (1 mark)
----------------- (1 mark for diagram)
The width L of the potential barrier that separates an α-
particle from the outside world depends on the particle’s
kinetic energy E. This width is the distance between the
point marked by the nuclear radius R, and the point, R0,
where an α-particle emerges on the other side of the barrier
-------------------- (1 mark)
At the distance the kinetic energy of the α-particle must
be equal to electrostatic Coulomb repulsion energy.
2𝑍𝑒2
𝐸=
4𝜋𝜀0𝑅0
********** 8 **********
--- page 9 ---
-------------------- (1 mark)
Substituting the value of R0
2𝑍𝑒2
𝐿= 4𝜋𝜀0𝐸−𝑅
------------------------ (1 mark)
Hence, higher the energy of α-particle, the narrower the width of the barrier that
it is to tunnel through. Also, the width of the potential barrier is the most
important parameter in tunneling probability. Thus, highly energetic α-particles
have a good chance to escape the nucleus, and, for such nuclei, the nuclear
disintegration half-life is short. However, this process is highly nonlinear,
meaning a small increase in the α-particle energy can exponentially increase the
tunneling probability. This explains why the half-life of polonium that emits 8-
MeV α-particles is only hundreds of milliseconds and the half-life of uranium
that emits 4-MeV α-particles is billions of years.

(iv).(b)   Describe microcanonical and grand canonical ensemble with the help of suitable   (6)
diagrams.
Solution:
Microcanonical ensemble: It is the collection of a large number of systems having
the same energy E, volume V and total number of particles N. This ensemble is
also often called the NVE ensemble, as each of these three quantities is a constant
of the ensemble. The individual systems of microcanonical ensemble must remain
totally isolated (i.e. they are separated by rigid, impermeable and well insulated
walls, unable to exchange energy or particles with its environment) in order to stay
in statistical equilibrium.
------------------------ (3 marks= 2marks for explanation + 1 mark for diagram)
Grand Canonical ensemble: It is an ensemble of a large number of essentially
independent systems having the same temperature T, volume V and chemical
potential 𝜇. In the grand canonical ensemble, both energy E and number of particle
N are variable. The individual systems of a grand canonical ensemble are separated
by rigid, permeable but conducting walls as shown in Fig. 4. As the separating
walls are conducting and permeable, the exchange of heat energy as well as the
particles between the systems takes place, bringing all the systems arrive at
common temperature T and chemical potential 𝜇. The chemical potential is the
energy required to add a particle to the system.
------------------------ (3 marks= 2marks for explanation + 1 mark for diagram)

Let us divide a large box into k cells having areas aj, a2, a3, ..., ax (Fig. 5). Then we throw n
identical balls such that no part of the box is favored. Each cell will contain different number of
balls. This is a distribution. If this experiment is repeated many times, it is found that certain
distribution occurs more frequently than others. This particular outcome is the most probable
distribution.

The probability W of a particular distribution depends on two factors:

i. The a priori probability (G) of the distribution which depends on the property of each
cell.
ii. The thermodynamic probability (Q) of the distribution which is the number of

different accessible sequences in which the balls may be distributed among the cells
without changing the number of balls in each cell. Thus we assume that the balls are
identical but distinguishable.

(v).(b)   Explain Planck’s distribution law for blackbody radiation in the light of statistical   (6)

Solution:

Let a black body cavity of volume V at temperature T K is filled with large number of
indistinguishable photons of various frequencies (photon gas). Photons have zero rest mass and
unit spin angular momentum (thus they are Bosons). Photons of same frequency are
indistinguishable and behave as a system of non-interacting particles.

To count the photons, we have to estimate the number of cells in the phase space (constructed
by positions and momentum of all photons). If each cell has the infinitesimal volume h’, then
the number of cells in the phase space where the momentum lies between p and p + dp is

g(p)dp _S sotydesoutt pfs
Where,
S, dxdydz = V = volume of the cavity, and
S, dp,dpydpz, = 4mp?dp = volume of the spherical cell of radius p and
thickness dp in the momentum space.
Therefore,

g(p)dp = = ‘dp

Thus, substituting the values of p and p + dp, we obtain the no of cells (quantum states) with
frequency between v and v + dv as

g(v)dv = anV Vdv

Since photon can have two types of volarzation and the fact that each cell can be occupied by
two photons of two different directions of polarizations, we multiply the above expression by
a factor 2 to obtain

g(v)dv = Svdv

The number of photons with energy between E and E + dE is

g(8)

Q.2.6   (20)
(vi).(a) State the four basic postulates of Maxwell-Boltzmann statistics. Write the   (10)
expression for Maxwell-Boltzmann distribution function. Give any two
applications and any two limitations of Maxwell-Boltzmann statistics.
Solution:
The basic postulates of MB statistics are
i. The associated particles are identical but distinguishable.
ii. Each energy state can contain any number of particles.
iii. Total number of particles in the entire system is constant.
iv. Total energy of all the particles in the entire system is constant.
v. Examples: gas molecules at high temperature and low pressure.

------------------------ (2 marks)
Any two applications of MB statistics   ------------------------- (2 marks)
The MB statistics is applicable to the ensembles of particles forming a dilute gas
(where the average separation between the molecules is large, individual molecules
could be distinguished) and also to free electrons in the conduction band of a
semiconductor at ordinary temperatures, provided the doping of the semiconductor
is not too high.
Any two limitations of MB statistics   ------------------------- (2 marks)
1. This statistics is applicable only to isolated gas molecular system in equilibrium,
where the potential energy due to the mutual interaction between the molecules is
negligible compared to their mean kinetic energy and the gas is dilute.
2. The expression for MB-count does not lead to the correct expression for entropy of an
ideal gas. It leads to what is known as Gibbs paradox, which can be resolved by dividing
the expression by N!.

(c) Explain the phenomenon of quantum tunnelling. State and explain the   (4)
mathematical expression of tunnelling probability of a particle in a potential
barrier. Also, identify the factors that increase the tunnelling probability.
Solution:
When both the width L and the height U0 of a potential barrier are finite, a part of
the quantum wave packet incident on one side of the barrier can penetrate the
barrier boundary and continue its motion inside the barrier, where it is gradually
attenuated on its way to the other side. A part of the incident quantum wave packet
eventually emerges on the other side of the barrier in the form of the transmitted
wave packet that tunneled through the barrier. This is termed as quantum tunneling.
---------------- (1 mark)
The tunneling probability T(L,E) is obtained as
where
Where E is the energy of the particle and U0 is the height of the potential barrier.
-------------------- (2 marks)
Tunneling probability depends on the barrier width and its height, and on the
energy of the quantum particle incident on the barrier.
---------------- (1 mark)
(d) Explain the concept of (a) macrostate, (b) microstate, (c) phase space and (d)   (4)
statistical ensemble.
Solution:
The macroscopic state or the macrostate of a system can be described by the
macroscopic parameters and the energy of the system such as temperature,
pressure, volume, density, etc.
---------------- (1 mark)
********** 2 **********
--- page 3 ---
The microscopic state or the microstate (also known as quantum state) of a system
can be specified by quoting the microscopic states such as position, momentum,
orientation, and configuration of the individual particles.
---------------- (1 mark)
This 6 numerical quantities required to specify the state of a particle in space at a
particular instant of time is defined as phase space.
---------------- (1 mark)
If we observe a system for a considerable period of time, recording its state at a
number of equal intervals of time (the number tending to infinity), we shall have a
collection of phase points. These phase points are supposed to represent an
identical system with the same macroscopic properties, but different microscopic
states. Thus we have a (infinitely) large number of systems similar in structure but
suitably randomized in microstates. This collection of imagined systems, which are
similar (i.e. macroscopically identical) but essentially independent and non-
interacting, is called ensemble.
---------------- (1 mark)
(e)   Explain (a) degrees of freedom with proper example (b) law of equipartition of   (4)
energy.
Solution:
The total number of independent quantities required to specify the configuration
and position of a system is called the number of degrees of freedom of the system.
The number is determined by the possibilities of motion of the parts of the system.
In the case of a gas, if we regard it as a point, its position is specified by the
coordinates x, y and z. thus each gas particle will have three degrees of freedom.
---------------- (2 marks)
The law of equipartition of energy states that the thermal equilibrium energy of the
gas particles is uniformly distributed among the various degrees of freedom and
for each of them it is (½)kT.
---------------- (2 marks)
Q.2.1   (20)
(i).(a) Define photoelectric effect and state all its five experimental results.   (10)
The work function for cadmium is 4.08 eV. What must be the wavelength of
radiation incident on cadmium so that the maximum velocity of photoelectrons
will be 7.2 x 105 m/s.
Solution:
A series of experiments performed in the late nineteenth century revealed that
electrons are emitted from a metal surface when light of sufficiently large
frequency (excepting alkali metals all other metals require ultraviolet rays) falls
upon it. This phenomenon is known as the photoelectric effect.
---------------- (1 mark)
The experimental results can be summarized as
1. It is an instantaneous process. The time lag between the incidence of light and
emission of electrons is ~ 10-9 s.
2. There is a threshold frequency for the emission of photoelectrons. If the light
beam contains no photons having frequencies higher than this threshold, no
********** 3 **********
--- page 4 ---
photoelectron will be emitted (in other words, there will be no photo current)
regardless of the intensity of the light source.
3. The threshold frequency varies with different metals.
4. The magnitude of the photocurrent is independent of the frequency of the light
but is directly proportional to the intensity of the light. However, the average
electron energy remains same.
5. For a given material, the higher the frequency of the light, the more energy the
photoelectrons have and the value of stopping potential also increases.
---------------- (5 marks)

(iii).(b) Explain “the half-life of polonium that emits 8-MeV α-particles is only hundreds
of milliseconds and the half-life of uranium that emits 4-MeV α-particles is   (6)
billions of years” by applying quantum mechanical tunnelling.
Solution:
Gamow explained the variation of half-lives by considering a ‘spherical-box’
model of the nucleus, where α-particles can bounce back and forth between the
walls as free particles. The confinement is provided by a strong nuclear potential
at a spherical wall of the box. The thickness of this wall, however, is not infinite
but finite, so in principle, a nuclear particle has a chance to escape this nuclear
confinement. On the inside wall of the confining barrier is a high nuclear potential
that keeps the α-particle in a small confinement. But when an α-particle gets out to
the other side of this wall, it is subject to electrostatic Coulomb repulsion and
moves away from the nucleus.
-------------------- (1 mark)
----------------- (1 mark for diagram)
The width L of the potential barrier that separates an α-
particle from the outside world depends on the particle’s
kinetic energy E. This width is the distance between the
point marked by the nuclear radius R, and the point, R0,
where an α-particle emerges on the other side of the barrier
-------------------- (1 mark)
At the distance the kinetic energy of the α-particle must
be equal to electrostatic Coulomb repulsion energy.
2𝑍𝑒2
𝐸=
4𝜋𝜀0𝑅0

--- page 9 ---
-------------------- (1 mark)
Substituting the value of R0
2𝑍𝑒2
𝐿= 4𝜋𝜀0𝐸−𝑅
------------------------ (1 mark)
Hence, higher the energy of α-particle, the narrower the width of the barrier that
it is to tunnel through. Also, the width of the potential barrier is the most
important parameter in tunneling probability. Thus, highly energetic α-particles
have a good chance to escape the nucleus, and, for such nuclei, the nuclear
disintegration half-life is short. However, this process is highly nonlinear,
meaning a small increase in the α-particle energy can exponentially increase the
tunneling probability. This explains why the half-life of polonium that emits 8-
MeV α-particles is only hundreds of milliseconds and the half-life of uranium
that emits 4-MeV α-particles is billions of years.

(iv).(b)   Describe microcanonical and grand canonical ensemble with the help of suitable   (6)
diagrams.
Solution:
Microcanonical ensemble: It is the collection of a large number of systems having
the same energy E, volume V and total number of particles N. This ensemble is
also often called the NVE ensemble, as each of these three quantities is a constant
of the ensemble. The individual systems of microcanonical ensemble must remain
totally isolated (i.e. they are separated by rigid, impermeable and well insulated
walls, unable to exchange energy or particles with its environment) in order to stay
in statistical equilibrium.
------------------------ (3 marks= 2marks for explanation + 1 mark for diagram)
Grand Canonical ensemble: It is an ensemble of a large number of essentially
independent systems having the same temperature T, volume V and chemical
potential 𝜇. In the grand canonical ensemble, both energy E and number of particle
N are variable. The individual systems of a grand canonical ensemble are separated
by rigid, permeable but conducting walls as shown in Fig. 4. As the separating
walls are conducting and permeable, the exchange of heat energy as well as the
particles between the systems takes place, bringing all the systems arrive at
common temperature T and chemical potential 𝜇. The chemical potential is the
energy required to add a particle to the system.
------------------------ (3 marks= 2marks for explanation + 1 mark for diagram)

(v).(a)   Find the mathematical formulation for the probability W of distribution for a   (10)

[Text in pictures on this page, read by OCR]
Let us divide a large box into k cells having areas aj, a2, a3, ..., ax (Fig. 5). Then we throw n
identical balls such that no part of the box is favored. Each cell will contain different number of
balls. This is a distribution. If this experiment is repeated many times, it is found that certain
distribution occurs more frequently than others. This particular outcome is the most probable
distribution.

The probability W of a particular distribution depends on two factors:

i. The a priori probability (G) of the distribution which depends on the property of each
cell.
ii. The thermodynamic probability (Q) of the distribution which is the number of

different accessible sequences in which the balls may be distributed among the cells
without changing the number of balls in each cell. Thus we assume that the balls are
identical but distinguishable.

indistinguishable and behave as a system of non-interacting particles.

To count the photons, we have to estimate the number of cells in the phase space (constructed
by positions and momentum of all photons). If each cell has the infinitesimal volume h’, then

Q.2.6   (20)
(vi).(a) State the four basic postulates of Maxwell-Boltzmann statistics. Write the   (10)
expression for Maxwell-Boltzmann distribution function. Give any two
applications and any two limitations of Maxwell-Boltzmann statistics.
Solution:
The basic postulates of MB statistics are
i. The associated particles are identical but distinguishable.
ii. Each energy state can contain any number of particles.
iii. Total number of particles in the entire system is constant.
iv. Total energy of all the particles in the entire system is constant.
v. Examples: gas molecules at high temperature and low pressure.

a

Above Eqn. is the Planck’s radiation formula in terms of frequency v, and has been successful
in explaining the black body radiation.
--- page 15 ---
------------------------ (2 marks)
Any two applications of MB statistics   ------------------------- (2 marks)
The MB statistics is applicable to the ensembles of particles forming a dilute gas
(where the average separation between the molecules is large, individual molecules
could be distinguished) and also to free electrons in the conduction band of a
semiconductor at ordinary temperatures, provided the doping of the semiconductor
is not too high.
Any two limitations of MB statistics   ------------------------- (2 marks)
1. This statistics is applicable only to isolated gas molecular system in equilibrium,
where the potential energy due to the mutual interaction between the molecules is
negligible compared to their mean kinetic energy and the gas is dilute.
2. The expression for MB-count does not lead to the correct expression for entropy of an
ideal gas. It leads to what is known as Gibbs paradox, which can be resolved by dividing
the expression by N!.

(c) Explain the phenomenon of quantum tunnelling. State and explain the   (4)
mathematical expression of tunnelling probability of a particle in a potential
barrier. Also, identify the factors that increase the tunnelling probability.
Solution:
When both the width L and the height U0 of a potential barrier are finite, a part of
the quantum wave packet incident on one side of the barrier can penetrate the
barrier boundary and continue its motion inside the barrier, where it is gradually
attenuated on its way to the other side. A part of the incident quantum wave packet
eventually emerges on the other side of the barrier in the form of the transmitted
wave packet that tunneled through the barrier. This is termed as quantum tunneling.
---------------- (1 mark)
The tunneling probability T(L,E) is obtained as
where
Where E is the energy of the particle and U0 is the height of the potential barrier.
-------------------- (2 marks)
Tunneling probability depends on the barrier width and its height, and on the
energy of the quantum particle incident on the barrier.
---------------- (1 mark)
(d) Explain the concept of (a) macrostate, (b) microstate, (c) phase space and (d)   (4)
statistical ensemble.
Solution:
The macroscopic state or the macrostate of a system can be described by the
macroscopic parameters and the energy of the system such as temperature,
pressure, volume, density, etc.
---------------- (1 mark)
********** 2 **********
--- page 3 ---
The microscopic state or the microstate (also known as quantum state) of a system
can be specified by quoting the microscopic states such as position, momentum,
orientation, and configuration of the individual particles.
---------------- (1 mark)
This 6 numerical quantities required to specify the state of a particle in space at a
particular instant of time is defined as phase space.
---------------- (1 mark)
If we observe a system for a considerable period of time, recording its state at a
number of equal intervals of time (the number tending to infinity), we shall have a
collection of phase points. These phase points are supposed to represent an
identical system with the same macroscopic properties, but different microscopic
states. Thus we have a (infinitely) large number of systems similar in structure but
suitably randomized in microstates. This collection of imagined systems, which are
similar (i.e. macroscopically identical) but essentially independent and non-
interacting, is called ensemble.
---------------- (1 mark)
(e)   Explain (a) degrees of freedom with proper example (b) law of equipartition of   (4)
energy.
Solution:
The total number of independent quantities required to specify the configuration
and position of a system is called the number of degrees of freedom of the system.
The number is determined by the possibilities of motion of the parts of the system.
In the case of a gas, if we regard it as a point, its position is specified by the
coordinates x, y and z. thus each gas particle will have three degrees of freedom.
---------------- (2 marks)
The law of equipartition of energy states that the thermal equilibrium energy of the
gas particles is uniformly distributed among the various degrees of freedom and
for each of them it is (½)kT.
---------------- (2 marks)
Q.2.1   (20)
(i).(a) Define photoelectric effect and state all its five experimental results.   (10)
The work function for cadmium is 4.08 eV. What must be the wavelength of
radiation incident on cadmium so that the maximum velocity of photoelectrons
will be 7.2 x 105 m/s.
Solution:
A series of experiments performed in the late nineteenth century revealed that
electrons are emitted from a metal surface when light of sufficiently large
frequency (excepting alkali metals all other metals require ultraviolet rays) falls
upon it. This phenomenon is known as the photoelectric effect.
---------------- (1 mark)
The experimental results can be summarized as
1. It is an instantaneous process. The time lag between the incidence of light and
emission of electrons is ~ 10-9 s.
2. There is a threshold frequency for the emission of photoelectrons. If the light
beam contains no photons having frequencies higher than this threshold, no
********** 3 **********
--- page 4 ---
photoelectron will be emitted (in other words, there will be no photo current)
regardless of the intensity of the light source.
3. The threshold frequency varies with different metals.
4. The magnitude of the photocurrent is independent of the frequency of the light
but is directly proportional to the intensity of the light. However, the average
electron energy remains same.
5. For a given material, the higher the frequency of the light, the more energy the
photoelectrons have and the value of stopping potential also increases.
---------------- (5 marks)

(iii).(b) Explain “the half-life of polonium that emits 8-MeV α-particles is only hundreds
of milliseconds and the half-life of uranium that emits 4-MeV α-particles is   (6)
billions of years” by applying quantum mechanical tunnelling.
Solution:
Gamow explained the variation of half-lives by considering a ‘spherical-box’
model of the nucleus, where α-particles can bounce back and forth between the
walls as free particles. The confinement is provided by a strong nuclear potential
at a spherical wall of the box. The thickness of this wall, however, is not infinite
but finite, so in principle, a nuclear particle has a chance to escape this nuclear
confinement. On the inside wall of the confining barrier is a high nuclear potential
that keeps the α-particle in a small confinement. But when an α-particle gets out to
the other side of this wall, it is subject to electrostatic Coulomb repulsion and
moves away from the nucleus.
-------------------- (1 mark)
----------------- (1 mark for diagram)
The width L of the potential barrier that separates an α-
particle from the outside world depends on the particle’s
kinetic energy E. This width is the distance between the
point marked by the nuclear radius R, and the point, R0,
where an α-particle emerges on the other side of the barrier
-------------------- (1 mark)
At the distance the kinetic energy of the α-particle must
be equal to electrostatic Coulomb repulsion energy.

4𝜋𝜀0𝑅0

-------------------- (1 mark)
Substituting the value of R0
2𝑍𝑒2
𝐿= 4𝜋𝜀0𝐸−𝑅
------------------------ (1 mark)
Hence, higher the energy of α-particle, the narrower the width of the barrier that
it is to tunnel through. Also, the width of the potential barrier is the most
important parameter in tunneling probability. Thus, highly energetic α-particles
have a good chance to escape the nucleus, and, for such nuclei, the nuclear
disintegration half-life is short. However, this process is highly nonlinear,
meaning a small increase in the α-particle energy can exponentially increase the
tunneling probability. This explains why the half-life of polonium that emits 8-
MeV α-particles is only hundreds of milliseconds and the half-life of uranium
that emits 4-MeV α-particles is billions of years.
------------------------ (1 mark)

(iv).(b)   Describe microcanonical and grand canonical ensemble with the help of suitable   (6)
diagrams.
Solution:
Microcanonical ensemble: It is the collection of a large number of systems having
the same energy E, volume V and total number of particles N. This ensemble is
also often called the NVE ensemble, as each of these three quantities is a constant
of the ensemble. The individual systems of microcanonical ensemble must remain
totally isolated (i.e. they are separated by rigid, impermeable and well insulated
walls, unable to exchange energy or particles with its environment) in order to stay
in statistical equilibrium.
------------------------ (3 marks= 2marks for explanation + 1 mark for diagram)
Grand Canonical ensemble: It is an ensemble of a large number of essentially
independent systems having the same temperature T, volume V and chemical
potential 𝜇. In the grand canonical ensemble, both energy E and number of particle
N are variable. The individual systems of a grand canonical ensemble are separated
by rigid, permeable but conducting walls as shown in Fig. 4. As the separating
walls are conducting and permeable, the exchange of heat energy as well as the
particles between the systems takes place, bringing all the systems arrive at
common temperature T and chemical potential 𝜇. The chemical potential is the
energy required to add a particle to the system.
------------------------ (3 marks= 2marks for explanation + 1 mark for diagram)

Let us divide a large box into k cells having areas aj, a2, a3, ..., ax (Fig. 5). Then we throw n
identical balls such that no part of the box is favored. Each cell will contain different number of
balls. This is a distribution. If this experiment is repeated many times, it is found that certain
distribution occurs more frequently than others. This particular outcome is the most probable
distribution.

The probability W of a particular distribution depends on two factors:

i. The a priori probability (G) of the distribution which depends on the property of each
cell.
ii. The thermodynamic probability (Q) of the distribution which is the number of

different accessible sequences in which the balls may be distributed among the cells
without changing the number of balls in each cell. Thus we assume that the balls are
identical but distinguishable.

Q.2.6   (20)
(vi).(a) State the four basic postulates of Maxwell-Boltzmann statistics. Write the   (10)
expression for Maxwell-Boltzmann distribution function. Give any two
applications and any two limitations of Maxwell-Boltzmann statistics.
Solution:
The basic postulates of MB statistics are
i. The associated particles are identical but distinguishable.
ii. Each energy state can contain any number of particles.
iii. Total number of particles in the entire system is constant.
iv. Total energy of all the particles in the entire system is constant.
v. Examples: gas molecules at high temperature and low pressure.

Above Eqn. is the Planck’s radiation formula in terms of frequency v, and has been successful
in explaining the black body radiation.
--- page 15 ---
------------------------ (2 marks)
Any two applications of MB statistics   ------------------------- (2 marks)
The MB statistics is applicable to the ensembles of particles forming a dilute gas
(where the average separation between the molecules is large, individual molecules
could be distinguished) and also to free electrons in the conduction band of a
semiconductor at ordinary temperatures, provided the doping of the semiconductor
is not too high.
Any two limitations of MB statistics   ------------------------- (2 marks)
1. This statistics is applicable only to isolated gas molecular system in equilibrium,
where the potential energy due to the mutual interaction between the molecules is
negligible compared to their mean kinetic energy and the gas is dilute.
2. The expression for MB-count does not lead to the correct expression for entropy of an
ideal gas. It leads to what is known as Gibbs paradox, which can be resolved by dividing
the expression by N!.

Q.1   (20)
(a) State and explain Stefan’s law of blackbody radiation. Calculate the energy (4)
radiated per minute per unit area by a black body, maintained at 127C.
Solution:
Josef Stefan based on experimental facts suggested that the total emissive power
of a blackbody (I) is proportional to the fourth power of the absolute temperature.
------------------ (2 marks)
Energy radiated per minute = 5.67 x 10-8 x (127 + 273)4 x 60 = 87.09 x 103 J/m2
------------------ (2 marks)
(b) Explain any four necessary conditions of well-behaved wave function.   (4)
Solution:
Any four conditions.   ------------------ (4 marks)
A wave-function Ψ is well-behaved (i.e., capable of yielding meaningful results)
if it satisfies the following conditions:
1. Ψ must be finite and continuous at all points in space. All its first and second-
order partial derivatives must also be finite and continuous everywhere. If Ψ is
infinite, it would imply an infinitely large probability of finding the particle in that
region which would be in violation to the uncertainty principle. Also, since Ψ is
related to a physical quantity, it cannot have a discontinuity at any point.
2. The function must be single-valued;   i.e. at any point in space, the
function ∫Ψ∗Ψ must have only one numerical value. This is because any physical
quantity can have only one value at a point.
3. Ψ must be normalizable, i.e., Ψ must go to zero as x, y, z tends to ±∞ in order
that ∫ ∞ |Ψ|2 𝑑𝑉= 𝑓𝑖𝑛𝑖𝑡𝑒 𝑛𝑢𝑚𝑏𝑒𝑟.
−∞   ∞
4. The wave functions must be orthogonal. i.e., ∫ Ψ𝑖 ∗Ψ𝑗 𝑑𝑉= 0 𝑤ℎ𝑒𝑛 𝑖≠𝑗.
−∞
********** 1 **********
--- page 2 ---
(c) Explain the concept of particle flux from the quantum mechanical point of view   (4)
and find its mathematical expression.
Solution:
If there are N particles per unit length and each one has speed u in the positive x-
direction, then all the particles in a length u ∆t will pass a fixed point in time
interval ∆t. The number of particles passing a fixed point per unit time is the
particle flux (F)given by
𝐹= 𝑁𝑢∆𝑡 ∆𝑡= 𝑁𝑢   (1)
------------------- (1 mark)
--------------------------- (2 marks)
The flux (F) is then given by
𝐹= |𝐴|2 ℏ𝑘
𝑚
------------------- (1 mark)
(d) Explain (a) postulate of equal a priori probability (b) thermodynamic probability,   (4)
(c) statistical ensemble and (d) phase space.
Solution:
The fundamental postulate of classical statistical mechanics (also known as the
postulate of equal a priori probability) states that for a macroscopic system in
equilibrium, all different microstates conforming to the macroscopic conditions of
the system are equally probable.
---------------- (1 mark)
Thermodynamic probability is defined as number of alternative microscopic
arrangements which corresponds to the same macroscopic state.
********** 2 **********

[Text in pictures on this page, read by OCR]
In quantum mechanics, if wavefunction y (x) represents a single particle, then p(x)=|w(x)/?
gives the probability of finding the particle at the position x. The wavefunction can also

represent more than one particle.

In particular, the wavefunction y(x) = A exp(ikx) can represent a set of particles, moving in
thex-direction, all with the same momentum hk. In this case. the position of any one particle
is not specified (to do so violates the Heisenberg uncertainty principle), but the average
number of particles per unit length is given by |y(x)/= [4/. In this context, ‘average’ means
the average of a large number of observations made on particles with this wavefunction. The

speed u is obtained from the momentum magnitude p according to the following relations
p=hkand u=p/m
so that: u=hk/m

Since k > 0, so is u.

where k is the wave-number.
--- page 3 ---
---------------- (1 mark)
If we observe a system for a considerable period of time, recording its state at a
number of equal intervals of time (the number tending to infinity), we shall have a
collection of phase points. These phase points are supposed to represent an
identical system with the same macroscopic properties, but different microscopic
states. Thus we have a (infinitely) large number of systems similar in structure but
suitably randomized in microstates. This collection of imagined systems, which are
similar (i.e. macroscopically identical) but essentially independent and non-
interacting, is called ensemble.
---------------- (1 mark)
This 6 numerical quantities required to specify the state of a particle in space at a
particular instant of time is defined as phase space.
---------------- (1 mark)
(e)   Which type of statistical distribution is applied to derive Planck’s radiation formula   (4)
with suitable reason?
Solution:
Bose-Einstein distribution is applied to derive Planck’s radiation formula.
---------------- (1 mark)
A black body cavity of volume V at temperature T K is filled with large number of
indistinguishable photons of various frequencies (photon gas). Photons have zero
rest mass and unit spin angular momentum (thus they are Bosons). Photons of same
frequency are indistinguishable and behave as a system of non-interacting
particles.
---------------- (3 marks)
Q.2.1   (20)
(i).(a) Explain the phenomenon of Compton effect and write the expressions for   (10)
momentum conservation and Compton shift with the help of a suitable diagram.
X-rays with initial wavelength 0.5 x 10-10 m undergo Compton scattering. For what
scattering angle is the wavelength of the scattered X-rays greater than that of the
incident X-rays by one percent?
Solution:
When short wavelength x-rays (photon) is incident on an element of low atomic
weight material like carbon (for example, graphite), there is a decrease in energy
of the photon (or, in other words, increase in wavelength)as a part of energy its
initial energy is transferred to the electrons of the material which is scattered.
This effect is called the Compton Effect or Compton Scattering.

(i).(b)   Write the Schrodinger's equation in its one-dimensional and three-dimensional   (6)

Properties of Schrodinger's equation:
1. Linearity: The Schrödinger equation is linear in the wave-function Ψ which
means that the equation does not involve higher powers of the wave-function or its
derivatives.
2. Superposition of eigenfunctions or eigenstates: The linearity of Schrödinger's
equation implies that a linear combination of solutions of Schrödinger's equation
for any system is also a solution. Hence, if Ψ1 and Ψ2 satisfy the Schrödinger's
equation then
Ψ = a1Ψ1 + a2Ψ2
is also a solution, where a1and a2are constants. Hence, the wave-functions Ψ1 and
Ψ2 obey the superposition principle.

The energy E1 is called the zero-point energy. Obviously, the energy is not zero in
this case. The zero-point energy is the consequence of the uncertainty principle. If
the energy of the particle is zero, its momentum is also zero. Then according to
uncertainty principle, the position of the particle would become infinite and the
particle cannot be confined to the box. Therefore, the particle cannot have zero

(iii).(b) Describe the construction and working of scanning tunneling microscope (STM)
with appropriate diagram.   (6)
Solution:
Construction – ( 4 marks = 2 marks for diagram + 2 marks for explanation)
Working – (2 marks)
Construction: The quantum tunneling phenomenon at metallic surfaces, is the
physical principle behind the operation of the scanning tunneling microscope
(STM), invented in 1981 by Gerd Binnig and Heinrich Rohrer. The STM device
consists of a scanning tip (a needle, usually made of tungsten, platinum-iridium,
or gold); a piezoelectric device that controls the tip’s elevation in a typical range
of 0.4 to 0.7 nm above the surface to be scanned; a device that controls the motion
of the tip along the surface; and a computer to display images. While the sample
is kept at a suitable voltage bias, the scanning tip moves along the surface and
the tunneling-electron current between the tip and the surface is registered at each
position.
Working: The amount of the current
depends on the probability of electron
tunneling from the surface to the tip,
which, in   turn, depends on the
elevation of the tip above the surface.
Hence,   at each   tip position, the
distance from the tip to the surface is
measured by measuring how many
electrons tunnel out from the surface to
the tip. This method can give an
unprecedented resolution of about 0.001 nm, which is about 1% of the average
diameter of an atom. When the strength of an external electric field is constant,
the tunneling-electron current has different values at different elevations of the
tip above the surface.

(iv).(b)   Describe canonical and grand canonical ensemble with the help of suitable   (6)
diagrams.
Solution:
Canonical ensembles: A canonical ensemble is the ensemble that represents the
collection of a large number of essentially independent systems having a fixed
volume V and same number of particles N and are thermal equilibrium with a heat
bath at a fixed temperature T (or with each other). The equality of temperature can
either be achieved by bringing each system in thermal contact with a large heat
reservoir at constant temperature T or bringing all of the systems in thermal contact
with one another. Therefore, the individual systems of a Canonical ensemble are
separated by rigid, impermeable but conducting walls. As the separating walls are
conducting, heat can be exchanged between the systems, bringing them to a
uniform temperature T.
------------------------ (3 marks= 2marks for explanation + 1 mark for diagram)
Grand Canonical ensemble: It is an ensemble of a large number of essentially
independent systems having the same temperature T, volume V and chemical
potential 𝜇. In the grand canonical ensemble, both energy E and number of particle
N are variable. The individual systems of a grand canonical ensemble are separated
by rigid, permeable but conducting walls as shown in Fig. 4. As the separating
walls are conducting and permeable, the exchange of heat energy as well as the
particles between the systems takes place, bringing all the systems arrive at
common temperature T and chemical potential 𝜇. The chemical potential is the
energy required to add a particle to the system.

Q.2.5   (20)
(v).(a)   State any four basic postulates of Bose-Einstein and Fermi-Dirac statistics. Write   (10)
the mathematical expression of the probability W for Bose-Einstein and Fermi-
Dirac statistics.
Solution:
The basic postulates of BE statistics are
1. The associated particles are identical and indistinguishable.
2. Each energy state can contain any number of particles.
3. Total energy and total number of particles of the entire system is constant.
4. The particles have zero or integral spin.
5. The wave function of the system is symmetric under the positional exchange of
any two particles. Such particles are known as Bosons. For example, photons,
phonons, all mesons, etc.
---------------- (Any 4 = 4 marks)
Basic features of FD statistics:
1. The particles (fermions) are identical and indistinguishable.
2. They obey Heisenberg’s uncertainty principle and the Pauli’s exclusion principle.
3. There is definite a priori restriction on the number of fermions in a quantum state.
There can be simultaneously no more than one particle in a quantum state (i.e. it
would either remain empty or can at best contain one fermion).
4. Total number and energy of particles in the entire system is constant.
---------------- (4 marks)
Probability W for Bose-Einstein

Let us divide a large box into k cells having areas aj, a2, a3, ..., ax (Fig. 5). Then we throw n
identical balls such that no part of the box is favored. Each cell will contain different number of
balls. This is a distribution. If this experiment is repeated many times, it is found that certain
distribution occurs more frequently than others. This particular outcome is the most probable
distribution.

The probability W of a particular distribution depends on two factors:

i. The a priori probability (G) of the distribution which depends on the property of each
cell.
ii. The thermodynamic probability (Q) of the distribution which is the number of

different accessible sequences in which the balls may be distributed among the cells
without changing the number of balls in each cell. Thus we assume that the balls are
identical but distinguishable.

The a priori probability gi that a ball falls in the i-th cell is equal to the fractional area of the cell.
If A is the total area, then

a= with Yg;=1 and Ya,=A

The a priori probability of two balls simultaneously in the i-th cell is (g;). The a priori probability
that nj balls go to i-th cell is (g;)™.

Q.1   (20)
(a) State and explain Stefan’s law of blackbody radiation. Calculate the energy (4)
radiated per minute per unit area by a black body, maintained at 127C.
Solution:
Josef Stefan based on experimental facts suggested that the total emissive power
of a blackbody (I) is proportional to the fourth power of the absolute temperature.
------------------ (2 marks)
Energy radiated per minute = 5.67 x 10-8 x (127 + 273)4 x 60 = 87.09 x 103 J/m2
------------------ (2 marks)
(b) Explain any four necessary conditions of well-behaved wave function.   (4)
Solution:
Any four conditions.   ------------------ (4 marks)
A wave-function Ψ is well-behaved (i.e., capable of yielding meaningful results)
if it satisfies the following conditions:
1. Ψ must be finite and continuous at all points in space. All its first and second-
order partial derivatives must also be finite and continuous everywhere. If Ψ is
infinite, it would imply an infinitely large probability of finding the particle in that
region which would be in violation to the uncertainty principle. Also, since Ψ is
related to a physical quantity, it cannot have a discontinuity at any point.
2. The function must be single-valued;   i.e. at any point in space, the
function ∫Ψ∗Ψ must have only one numerical value. This is because any physical
quantity can have only one value at a point.
3. Ψ must be normalizable, i.e., Ψ must go to zero as x, y, z tends to ±∞ in order
that ∫ ∞ |Ψ|2 𝑑𝑉= 𝑓𝑖𝑛𝑖𝑡𝑒 𝑛𝑢𝑚𝑏𝑒𝑟.
−∞   ∞
4. The wave functions must be orthogonal. i.e., ∫ Ψ𝑖 ∗Ψ𝑗 𝑑𝑉= 0 𝑤ℎ𝑒𝑛 𝑖≠𝑗.
−∞
********** 1 **********
--- page 2 ---
(c) Explain the concept of particle flux from the quantum mechanical point of view   (4)
and find its mathematical expression.
Solution:
If there are N particles per unit length and each one has speed u in the positive x-
direction, then all the particles in a length u ∆t will pass a fixed point in time
interval ∆t. The number of particles passing a fixed point per unit time is the
particle flux (F)given by
𝐹= 𝑁𝑢∆𝑡 ∆𝑡= 𝑁𝑢   (1)
------------------- (1 mark)
--------------------------- (2 marks)
The flux (F) is then given by
𝐹= |𝐴|2 ℏ𝑘
𝑚
------------------- (1 mark)
(d) Explain (a) postulate of equal a priori probability (b) thermodynamic probability,   (4)
(c) statistical ensemble and (d) phase space.
Solution:
The fundamental postulate of classical statistical mechanics (also known as the
postulate of equal a priori probability) states that for a macroscopic system in
equilibrium, all different microstates conforming to the macroscopic conditions of
the system are equally probable.
---------------- (1 mark)
Thermodynamic probability is defined as number of alternative microscopic
arrangements which corresponds to the same macroscopic state.
********** 2 **********

[Text in pictures on this page, read by OCR]
In quantum mechanics, if wavefunction y (x) represents a single particle, then p(x)=|w(x)/?
gives the probability of finding the particle at the position x. The wavefunction can also

represent more than one particle.

In particular, the wavefunction y(x) = A exp(ikx) can represent a set of particles, moving in
thex-direction, all with the same momentum hk. In this case. the position of any one particle
is not specified (to do so violates the Heisenberg uncertainty principle), but the average
number of particles per unit length is given by |y(x)/= [4/. In this context, ‘average’ means
the average of a large number of observations made on particles with this wavefunction. The

speed u is obtained from the momentum magnitude p according to the following relations
p=hkand u=p/m
so that: u=hk/m

Since k > 0, so is u.

where k is the wave-number.
--- page 3 ---
---------------- (1 mark)
If we observe a system for a considerable period of time, recording its state at a
number of equal intervals of time (the number tending to infinity), we shall have a
collection of phase points. These phase points are supposed to represent an
identical system with the same macroscopic properties, but different microscopic
states. Thus we have a (infinitely) large number of systems similar in structure but
suitably randomized in microstates. This collection of imagined systems, which are
similar (i.e. macroscopically identical) but essentially independent and non-
interacting, is called ensemble.
---------------- (1 mark)
This 6 numerical quantities required to specify the state of a particle in space at a
particular instant of time is defined as phase space.
---------------- (1 mark)
(e)   Which type of statistical distribution is applied to derive Planck’s radiation formula   (4)
with suitable reason?
Solution:

This effect is called the Compton Effect or Compton Scattering.

Properties of Schrodinger's equation:
1. Linearity: The Schrödinger equation is linear in the wave-function Ψ which
means that the equation does not involve higher powers of the wave-function or its
derivatives.
2. Superposition of eigenfunctions or eigenstates: The linearity of Schrödinger's
equation implies that a linear combination of solutions of Schrödinger's equation
for any system is also a solution. Hence, if Ψ1 and Ψ2 satisfy the Schrödinger's
equation then
Ψ = a1Ψ1 + a2Ψ2
is also a solution, where a1and a2are constants. Hence, the wave-functions Ψ1 and
Ψ2 obey the superposition principle.

The energy E1 is called the zero-point energy. Obviously, the energy is not zero in
this case. The zero-point energy is the consequence of the uncertainty principle. If
the energy of the particle is zero, its momentum is also zero. Then according to
uncertainty principle, the position of the particle would become infinite and the
particle cannot be confined to the box. Therefore, the particle cannot have zero
energy.

(iii).(b) Describe the construction and working of scanning tunneling microscope (STM)
with appropriate diagram.   (6)
Solution:
Construction – ( 4 marks = 2 marks for diagram + 2 marks for explanation)
Working – (2 marks)
Construction: The quantum tunneling phenomenon at metallic surfaces, is the
physical principle behind the operation of the scanning tunneling microscope
(STM), invented in 1981 by Gerd Binnig and Heinrich Rohrer. The STM device
consists of a scanning tip (a needle, usually made of tungsten, platinum-iridium,
or gold); a piezoelectric device that controls the tip’s elevation in a typical range
of 0.4 to 0.7 nm above the surface to be scanned; a device that controls the motion
of the tip along the surface; and a computer to display images. While the sample
is kept at a suitable voltage bias, the scanning tip moves along the surface and
the tunneling-electron current between the tip and the surface is registered at each
position.
Working: The amount of the current
depends on the probability of electron
tunneling from the surface to the tip,
which, in   turn, depends on the
elevation of the tip above the surface.
Hence,   at each   tip position, the
distance from the tip to the surface is
measured by measuring how many
electrons tunnel out from the surface to
the tip. This method can give an
unprecedented resolution of about 0.001 nm, which is about 1% of the average
diameter of an atom. When the strength of an external electric field is constant,
the tunneling-electron current has different values at different elevations of the
tip above the surface.

---------------- (1.5 mark)
(iv).(b)   Describe canonical and grand canonical ensemble with the help of suitable   (6)
diagrams.
Solution:
Canonical ensembles: A canonical ensemble is the ensemble that represents the
collection of a large number of essentially independent systems having a fixed
volume V and same number of particles N and are thermal equilibrium with a heat
bath at a fixed temperature T (or with each other). The equality of temperature can
either be achieved by bringing each system in thermal contact with a large heat
reservoir at constant temperature T or bringing all of the systems in thermal contact
with one another. Therefore, the individual systems of a Canonical ensemble are
separated by rigid, impermeable but conducting walls. As the separating walls are
conducting, heat can be exchanged between the systems, bringing them to a
uniform temperature T.
------------------------ (3 marks= 2marks for explanation + 1 mark for diagram)
Grand Canonical ensemble: It is an ensemble of a large number of essentially
independent systems having the same temperature T, volume V and chemical
potential 𝜇. In the grand canonical ensemble, both energy E and number of particle
N are variable. The individual systems of a grand canonical ensemble are separated
by rigid, permeable but conducting walls as shown in Fig. 4. As the separating
walls are conducting and permeable, the exchange of heat energy as well as the
particles between the systems takes place, bringing all the systems arrive at
common temperature T and chemical potential 𝜇. The chemical potential is the
energy required to add a particle to the system.
